Donald Trump made another statement after the riot on January 7, 2021.

These paragraphs are an excerpt from [Domestic reactions to the January 6 United States Capitol attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack\&action=edit)]

In the aftermath of the attack, after drawing widespread condemnation from the [U.S. Congress](31756.md), members of [his administration](52231341.md), and the media, 45th [U.S. president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._President) [Donald Trump](4848272.md) released a video-recorded statement on January 7, reportedly to stop the resignations of his staff and the threats of impeachment or removal from office. In the statement, he condemned the violence at the [U.S. Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Capitol), saying that "a new administration will be inaugurated", which was widely seen as a concession, and his "focus now turns to ensuring a smooth, orderly, and seamless transition of power" to the [Joe Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_administration).[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_24_hours_later-585] *[Vanity Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanity_Fair_\(magazine\))* reported that Trump was at least partially convinced to make the statement by [U.S. senator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Senator) [Lindsey Graham](363279.md) (R-SC), who told Trump a sufficient number of Senate Republicans would support removing him from office unless he conceded.[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_peril-586] [Kayleigh McEnany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kayleigh_McEnany), the [White House press secretary](284101.md), had attempted to distance the administration from the rioters' behavior in a televised statement earlier in the day.[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_distance_administration-587] On January 9, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported that Trump had told [White House](33057.md) aides he regretted committing to an orderly transition of power and would never resign from office.[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_NYTPIIPII-588] In a March 25 interview on [Fox News](11121.md), Trump defended the Capitol attackers, saying they were patriots who posed "zero threat", and he criticized law enforcement for "persecuting" the rioters.[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_defends_rioters-589]

The [Joint Chiefs of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Chiefs_of_Staff) issued a statement on January 12 condemning the attack and reminding military personnel everywhere that incoming president [Joe Biden](145422.md) was about to become their commander-in-chief, saying "... the rights of freedom of speech and assembly do not give anyone the right to resort to violence, sedition, and insurrection".[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_JointChiefs-590] The statement also said, "As we have done throughout our history, the U.S. military will obey lawful orders from civilian leadership, support civilian authorities to protect lives and property, ensure public safety in accordance with the law, and remain fully committed to protecting and defending the Constitution of the United States against all enemies, foreign and domestic".[^cite_note-591][^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_officers_condemn-592] U.S. senator [Mitch McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_McConnell) ([R](32070.md)–[KY](16846.md)), then the [Senate majority leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Majority_Leader), called it a "failed insurrection",[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_smh-provoked-593][^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_cspan-mcconnell-insurrection-594] that "the mob was fed lies", and "they were provoked by the president and other powerful people".[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_smh-provoked-593] [Christopher Wray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_A._Wray), the director of the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) (FBI) since 2017, later characterized the incident as [domestic terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_terrorism_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-595][^cite_note-596] President Biden, who described the rioters as "terrorists" aimed at "overturning the will of the American people" later shared this opinion.[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_aljazeera6august-597] In early 2021, the [RAND Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAND_Corporation) released a framework to reduce the risk of extremist activity in the U.S. military.[^cite_note-598]

House speaker [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi) had the flags at the Capitol lowered to [half-staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-staff) in honor of [Brian Sicknick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Brian_Sicknick), a [United States Capitol Police](1915214.md) officer who died following the attacks.[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_nyt-2021-01-09-haberman-schmidt-599][^cite_note-600] Trump initially declined to lower flags at the White House or other federal buildings under his control, before changing his mind four days later.[^cite_note-601][^cite_note-602][^cite_note-603] Biden, [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence), and Pelosi offered condolences to Sicknick's family; Trump did not.[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_nyt-2021-01-09-haberman-schmidt-599][^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_nbc-2021-01-11-fitzsimons-604] After Sicknick's death, Senator [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz) ([R-TX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_of_Texas)) received backlash for previous speeches that were perceived as calls for violence.[^cite_note-605]

A survey by the Hobby School of Public Affairs at the [University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston) taken January 12–20 showed that nearly a third (32%) of Texas Republicans supported the attack, although overall 83% of all Texans who expressed an opinion were opposed to it.[^cite_note-606] In a poll of Americans just after the attack, 79% of those surveyed said America is "[falling apart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_decline)".[^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_axios_Republicans_and-607][^cite_note-Domestic_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_theh_4_in_5_say-608] In February 2022, the [Republican National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_National_Committee) called the events of January 6 "legitimate political discourse".[^cite_note-609]

The US art world reacted through the chronicling of the day as well as the creation of new work. Starting January 7, 2021, the [Smithsonian Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smithsonian_Museum) enacted its "rapid-response protocol" to gather rally signs, posters, flags, and weapons abandoned on the [National Mall](167796.md) and began work on a digital arts exhibit.[^cite_note-610] Visual artist [Paul Chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Chan_\(artist\)) created his "A drawing as a recording of an insurrection", a 163-inch (4.1 m) double-sided drawing[^cite_note-611] exhibited at the [Greene Naftali Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greene_Naftali_Gallery) in New York.[^cite_note-612] In December 2022, [literary press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_English-language_literary_presses) [Whiskey Tit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whiskey_Tit_Press) released *[Tell Me What You See](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tell_Me_What_You_See_\(2022_book\))*, the first fiction published about the attack.[^cite_note-613] At the one-year anniversary, One Six Comics published graphic novel series *1/6*[^cite_note-614] with an accompanying education and action guide by the Western States Center.[^cite_note-615] *The Society of Classical Poets* website posted various poems about the day,[^cite_note-616] including one glorifying deceased rioter [Ashli Babbitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashli_Babbitt).[^cite_note-617]

Biden, Harris, civil rights groups and celebrities immediately criticized the Capitol Police for a perceived "double standard" in the treatment of the protesters and rioters, who were mostly white. Biden stated, "No one can tell me that if it had been a group of Black Lives Matter protesting yesterday they wouldn't have been treated very, very differently than the mob of thugs that stormed the Capitol. We all know that's true and it is unacceptable". Harris stated, "We witnessed two systems of justice when we saw one that let extremists storm the United States Capitol and another that released tear gas on peaceful protestors ([Black Lives Matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Lives_Matter)) last summer".[^cite_note-618] [Michelle Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Obama) wrote, "Yesterday made it painfully clear that certain Americans are, in fact, allowed to denigrate the flag and symbols of our nation. They've just got to look the right way".[^cite_note-619][^cite_note-620] Capitol Police chief Steven Sund, who later resigned, explained they had prepared for a peaceful protest but were overwhelmed by an "angry, violent mob".[^cite_note-621] Later in the year, at a White House ceremony to thank officers who responded to the attack that day, Biden and Harris congratulated the police on their response, calling them "heroes".[^cite_note-622][^cite_note-623]

### International reactions

These paragraphs are an excerpt from [International reactions to the January 6 United States Capitol attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=International_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack\&action=edit)]

More than seventy countries and international organizations expressed their concerns over the attack and condemned the violence, with some specifically condemning President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s own role in inciting the attack.[^cite_note-624][^cite_note-International_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_\:4tao-625] Foreign leaders, diplomats, politicians, and institutions expressed shock, outrage, and condemnation of the events.[^cite_note-International_reactions_to_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_\:0-626][^cite_note-627] Multiple world leaders made a call for peace, describing the assault as "an attack on democracy".[^cite_note-628] The leaders of some countries, including [Brazil](3383.md), [Poland](22936.md), and [Hungary](13275.md), declined to condemn the situation, and described it as an internal U.S. affair.[^cite_note-629]

As early as January 2021, a few European security officials described the events as an attempted coup.[^cite_note-630]

### 14th Amendment disqualification

Main article: [2024 presidential eligibility of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_presidential_eligibility_of_Donald_Trump)

In late 2022 Trump [announced his candidacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2024_presidential_campaign) for the [2024 presidential election](59568546.md). Some legal scholars argued that Trump should be [barred from presidential office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_presidential_eligibility_of_Donald_Trump) under [section 3](31666.md#Section_3:_Disqualification_from_office_for_insurrection_or_rebellion) of the [Fourteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution](31666.md) because of his apparent support for the attack.[^cite_note-631] Three states, [Maine](19977.md), [Colorado](5399.md), and [Illinois](14849.md), issued rulings to disqualify Trump from appearing on election ballots, with Trump appealing in *[Trump v. Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_v._Anderson)*. The [Supreme Court](31737.md) unanimously ruled on March 4, 2024, that states could not remove Trump from the ballot.[^cite_note-politicoMarch4-20] Following [Biden's withdrawal from the race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election) in July, Trump ultimately defeated Kamala Harris the election in November, being [inaugurated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump) for a [second term](78298108.md) on January 20, 2025, with [JD Vance](53396477.md) as his vice president.

Other public officials involved in the January 6 attack have also faced disqualification under the Fourteenth Amendment. [Otero County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otero_County,_New_Mexico), New Mexico, commissioner [Couy Griffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Couy_Griffin) was disqualified and removed from office while Congressional representative [Marjorie Taylor Greene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marjorie_Taylor_Greene) survived a similar challenge.[^cite_note-632]

### Sarbanes–Oxley Act prosecutions

Over 350 defendants, including Trump, were charged with obstructing an official proceeding under the 2002 [Sarbanes-Oxley Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbanes-Oxley_Act). Prior to the January 6 prosecutions, such charges had never been brought in cases that did not involve evidence tampering. In *[Fischer v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fischer_v._United_States_\(2024\))*, the U.S. Supreme Court ruled 6–3 on non-ideological lines in favor of defendant Joseph Fischer and found that the obstruction charges in the case were overbroad, as they did not apply to evidence tampering, although charges against Trump could potentially proceed.[^cite_note-hillsox-633][^cite_note-634][^cite_note-635] Soon after the ruling, other January 6 criminal cases were reopened to adhere to the *Fischer* ruling and further usage of obstruction charges against January 6 defendants was stopped.[^cite_note-hillsox-633][^cite_note-nytsox-636]

In 2025, the U.S. Attorney's Office for the District of Columbia initiated an internal review of its prosecutions of January 6 defendants under the Sarbanes-Oxley Act.[^cite_note-637][^cite_note-nytsox-636]

### 2025 pardons and commutations

Main article: [Pardon of January 6 United States Capitol attack defendants](78937582.md)



On January 20, 2025, U.S. president Donald Trump signed executive order granting pardons and commutations to over 1,500 individuals involved in the January 6 attack.

On January 20, 2025, on his last day in office, U.S. president Joe Biden granted pardons to all members of the [House Select Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Select_Committee_on_the_January_6_Attack) that had investigated the January 6 attack, as well as their staff and the officers who testified.[^cite_note-638] Later that day, on the first day of [his second term](78298108.md), U.S. president Donald Trump issued a proclamation granting [clemency](288593.md) to approximately 1,200 individuals convicted for their involvement in the January 6 attack on the U.S. Capitol, and dismissing the cases of the remaining approximately 300 charged individuals. The pardon extended to those charged with vandalism and assaults on law enforcement officers, including members of the [Proud Boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proud_Boys) and the [Oath Keepers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_Keepers).[^cite_note-Feuer_2025-21] Following the pardons, Trump ordered the DOJ to purge previously published press releases about the arrests and convictions of those pardoned.[^cite_note-639] Soon after, video evidence of the attack likewise began being purged from government databases.[^cite_note-640]

## Analysis and terminology

See also: [Description of the attempts to overturn the election as an attempted coup](65905202.md#Description_as_attempted_coup_during_or_after_the_Capitol_attack)

This section is an excerpt from [Aftermath of the January 6 United States Capitol attack § Contemporary analysis and terminology](66369984.md#Contemporary_analysis_and_terminology).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack\&action=edit)]

A week following the attack, journalists were searching for an appropriate word to describe the event.[^cite_note-641] According to the Associated Press, U.S. media outlets first described the developments on January 6 as "a rally or protest", but as the events of the day escalated and further reporting and images emerged, the descriptions shifted to "an assault, a riot, an insurrection, domestic terrorism or even a [coup](25914154.md) attempt".[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_Bauder-642] It was variably observed that the media outlets were settling on the terms "riot" and "insurrection".[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_Bauder-642][^cite_note-643]

According to [NPR](29697232.md), "By definition, 'insurrection', and its derivative, 'insurgency', are accurate. 'Riot' and 'mob' are equally correct. While these words are not interchangeable, they are all suitable when describing Jan. 6."[^cite_note-644] *The New York Times* assessed the event as having brought the United States "hours away from a full-blown constitutional crisis".[^cite_note-645] [Brian Stelter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Stelter) in *CNN Business* wrote that the events of the Capitol attack "will be remembered as an act of domestic terrorism against the United States".[^cite_note-646] Although several of the rioters were charged with the crime of "[seditious conspiracy](26851299.md)", none of them were criminally charged with the crime of "[insurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confiscation_Act_of_1862#Insurrection_or_rebellion)", including Trump.[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_insur1-647][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_insur2-648]

The attack was widely described as an attempted [coup d'état](25914154.md)[^cite_note-649][^cite_note-650][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_coup_d-651] or [self-coup](1221872.md).[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_ProvostHarvey-652][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_Self-coup_journal-653][^cite_note-654] Federal judge [David Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_O._Carter) described Trump's actions as "a coup in search of a legal theory".[^cite_note-Broadwater_Feuer_Haberman_3/28/2022-655] [Naunihal Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naunihal_Singh_\(academic\)) of the U.S. [Naval War College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_War_College), and author of *Seizing Power: The Strategic Logic of Military Coups*, wrote that the attack on the Capitol was "an insurrection, a violent uprising against the government" and "sedition", but not a coup because Trump did not order the military "to seize power on his behalf".[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_wRwC4-656][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_6OT6q-657] The Coup D'état Project of the Cline Center for Advanced Social Research at the [University of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Illinois), which tracks coups and coup attempts globally, classified the attack as an "attempted dissident coup", defined as an unsuccessful coup attempt "initiated by a small group of discontents" such as "ex-military leaders, religious leaders, former government leaders, members of a legislature/parliament, and civilians \[but not police or the military]". The Cline Center said the "organized, illegal attempt to intervene in the presidential transition" by displacing Congress met this definition.[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_ClintCenterCNN-658][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_ClintCenterStmt-659] Some political scientists identified the attack as an attempted [self-coup](1221872.md), in which the [head of government](37570.md) attempts to strong-arm the other branches of government to entrench power.[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_UiQHf-660] Academic [Fiona Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiona_Hill_\(presidential_advisor\)), a former member of Trump's [National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._National_Security_Council), described the attack, and Trump's actions in the months leading up to it, as an attempted self-coup.[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_IDgK3-661] Experts have differed about whether the events on January 6 amounted to a coup or the like, some say yes,[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_ayeone-662][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_ayetwo-663][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_ayethree-664] some say no.[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_nayone-665][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_naytwo-666] News reporting about scholarly opinions reflects that division.[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_Zeitz-667]

The FBI classified the attack as domestic terrorism.[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_GHvJC-668][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_L4naB-669] At the Senate Judiciary Committee meeting on March 2, 2021, Wray testified:

> I was appalled, like you, at the violence and destruction that we saw that day. I was appalled that you, our country's elected leaders, were victimized right here in these very halls. That attack, that siege was criminal behavior, plain and simple, and it's behavior that we, the FBI, view as domestic terrorism. It's got no place in our democracy and tolerating it would make a mockery of our nation's rule of law.[^cite_note-670][^cite_note-671]

The [Congressional Research Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Research_Service) also concluded that the attack met the federal definition of domestic terrorism.[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_RA0uX-672][^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_uIhHG-673] Republican senator [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz) characterized it as terrorism at least eighteen times over the ensuing year, though he was among the Senate Republicans who blocked a bipartisan [January 6 commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_6_commission) to investigate it.[^cite_note-674][^cite_note-675]

On January 4, 2021, Steve Bannon, while discussing the planning for the upcoming events and speech by Trump on January 6 at The Ellipse, described it as a "bloodless coup".[^cite_note-CNN_7/15/2022-113][^cite_note-Peltz_10/29/2021-114]

A March 2023 poll found that 20.5 percent of respondents believed that violence to achieve a political goal is sometimes justified. Nearly 12 percent expressed their willingness to use force to restore Trump to power.[^cite_note-676] A June 2023 poll found that about 12 million American adults, or 4.4 percent of the adult population, believed violence is justified in returning Trump to the White House.[^cite_note-677]

### Historians' perspectives

For broader coverage of this topic, see [Timeline of violent incidents at the United States Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_violent_incidents_at_the_United_States_Capitol).

See also: [List of incidents of political violence in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_incidents_of_political_violence_in_Washington,_D.C.); [List of incidents of civil unrest in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_incidents_of_civil_unrest_in_the_United_States); [List of rebellions in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rebellions_in_the_United_States); and [List of attacks on legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_attacks_on_legislatures)



A commemorative plaque

[^cite_note-2026_PlaqueForJanuary6-678]

&#x20;honoring those who protected the US Capitol on January 6 sat for years out of sight in the Capitol basement, surrounded by maintenance equipment.

[^cite_note-WashPost_20260311-679]

&#x20;A 2022 law mandating the plaque stated it must be placed "at a permanent location on the western front".

[^cite_note-WashPost_20260311-679]

&#x20;In March 2026, more than five years after the attack, it was placed on a wall inside the Capitol, in an area closed to all tours and accessible by visitors only if they are escorted.

[^cite_note-WashPost_20260311-679]

While there have been other instances of violence at the Capitol in the 19th and 20th centuries, this event was the most severe assault on the building since the 1814 [burning of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_of_Washington) by [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) forces during the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812). The last attempt on the life of the vice president was a bomb plot against [Thomas Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_R._Marshall) in July 1915.[^cite_note-680] For the first time in U.S. history, a Confederate battle flag was flown inside the Capitol. The [Confederate States Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_Army) had never reached the Capitol, nor come closer than 6 miles (10 km) from the Capitol at the [Battle of Fort Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Fort_Stevens), during the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War).[^cite_note-list2-450][^cite_note-683]

[Douglas Brinkley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Brinkley), a historian at [Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University),[^cite_note-684] remarked on how January 6 would be remembered in [American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_States): "Now every January 6, we're going to have to remember what happened ... I worry if we lose the date that it will lose some of its wallop over time". He also wrote about Trump's responsibility during the attack: "There are always going to be puzzle pieces added to what occurred on January 6, because the president of the United States was sitting there watching this on television in the White House, as we all know, allowing it to go on and on".[^cite_note-685]

On the first anniversary of the attack, historians [Doris Kearns Goodwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doris_Kearns_Goodwin) and [Jon Meacham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Meacham) warned that the U.S. remained at "a crucial turning point". Meacham commented, "What you saw a year ago today was the worst instincts of both human nature and American politics and it's either a step on the way to the abyss or it is a call to arms figuratively for citizens to engage".[^cite_note-686]

[Robert Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Paxton) considered the attack to be evidence that Trump's movement was an example of [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism), a characterization that Paxton had resisted up to that point. Paxton compared the event to the French [6 February 1934 crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6_February_1934_crisis).[^cite_note-687]

[Richard J. Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_J._Evans) said that it was not a coup, but that it did represent a danger to democracy in the U.S.[^cite_note-688]

Other scholars expressed concern about how history would portray the attack and its aftermath.[^cite_note-689] [Larry Sabato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Sabato), director of the University of Virginia's Center for Politics, stated that reframing the insurrection as a "sightseeing tour" by the GOP has given "the far-right extremists, the neo-Nazi white supremacists who are obsessed with January 6, the counter reality they've been looking for of a bunch of patriots taking a tour in the Capitol".[^cite_note-690]

A [memorial plaque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_plaque) honoring Capitol police officers who were injured that day was installed at 4 a.m. on Saturday morning March 7, 2026.[^cite_note-691][^cite_note-692]

### Timeline

As with the Beer Hall Putsch, a would-be leader tried to take advantage of an already scheduled event (in Hitler's case, Kahr's speech; in Trump's, Congress's tallying of the electoral votes) to create a dramatic moment with himself at the center of attention, calling for bold action to upend the political order. Unlike Hitler's coup attempt, Trump already held top of office, so he was attempting to hold onto power, not seize it (the precise term for Trump's intended action is a 'self-coup' or 'autogolpe'). Thus, Trump was able to plan for the event well in advance, and with much greater control, including developing the legal arguments that could be used to justify rejecting the election's results.Pion-Berlin, David; Bruneau, Thomas; Goetze, Richard B. (2022). ["The Trump Self-Coup Attempt: Comparisons and Civil–Military Relations"](https://doi.org/10.1017%2Fgov.2022.13). *Government and Opposition*. **58** (4): 789–806. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1017/gov.2022.13](https://doi.org/10.1017%2Fgov.2022.13). [^cite_note-649]: &#x20;*Eastman v Thompson, et al.*, [ 8\:22-cv-00099-DOC-DFM Document 260](https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cacd.841840/gov.uscourts.cacd.841840.260.0.pdf), 44 (S.D. If Dr. Eastman and President Trump's plan had worked, it would have permanently ended the peaceful transition of power, undermining American democracy and the Constitution. If the country does not commit to investigating and pursuing accountability for those responsible, the Court fears January 6 will repeat itself.").

[^cite_note-Aftermath_of_the_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_ProvostHarvey-652]: &#x20;Harvey, Michael (2022). "Introduction: History's Rhymes". In Harvey, Michael (ed.). *Donald Trump in Historical Perspective*. New York: Routledge. p. 3. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.4324/9781003110361-1](https://doi.org/10.4324%2F9781003110361-1). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-003-11036-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-003-11036-1). As with the Beer Hall Putsch, a would-be leader tried to take advantage of an already scheduled event (in Hitler's case, Kahr's speech; in Trump's, Congress's tallying of the electoral votes) to create a dramatic moment with himself at the center of attention, calling for bold action to upend the political order. Unlike Hitler's coup attempt, Trump already held top of office, so he was attempting to hold onto power, not seize it (the precise term for Trump's intended action is a 'self-coup' or 'autogolpe'). Thus, Trump was able to plan for the event well in advance, and with much greater control, including developing the legal arguments that could be used to justify rejecting the election's results.
